### canonical_json is NON-deterministic for sets/frozensets containing NaN floats — violates the documented order-independence contract for unordered containers

- canonical_json's contract (module docstring lines 81-99 and _canonicalise docstring lines 119-123) promises that sets/frozensets 'whose elements are the same regardless of the order they were inserted' encode identically because unordered containers are sorted. ### Large worker results deadlock: parent joins the child before draining the result queue (classic mp.Queue feeder-pipe deadlock)
- **Location:** `src/haute/_worker_isolation.py:211-235 (run_isolated_worker) with _wait_for_worker:327-344 and _read_worker_payload:302-314`  ·  **confidence:** high  ·  **type:** bug  ·  bucket B059
- run_isolated_worker fully waits for the child to exit (_wait_for_worker loops on process.join until not process.is_alive()) BEFORE it ever reads the result queue (_read_worker_payload -> result_queue.get). With multiprocessing.Queue, the child's put() hands the serialized object to a background feeder thread that writes it into an OS pipe with a finite buffer (~64 KiB on Linux). If the pickled result exceeds the pipe buffer, the feeder blocks on the pipe write until the parent reads. But the parent never reads during the wait phase; it only join()s. At interpreter shutdown the child blocks in Queue._finalize_join waiting for the feeder, so the child never exits, and the parent's join() never...
- *Evidence:* Order in run_isolated_worker: (1) _wait_for_worker(process, worker_config) at line 211 -> while process.is_alive(): ...; process.join(timeout=wait_seconds) (lines 329-344), parent NEVER calls result_queue.get here. (2) only after the loop returns does line 213 call _read_worker_payload -> result_queue.get(timeout=1.0) (line 309). result_queue = ctx.Queue(maxsize=1) (line 194) with no cancel_join_t...

The result has two `dependencies` keys in one table; tomllib rejects it with `Cannot overwrite a value`, and `haute init` crashes (exit 1, raw traceback).
- *Evidence:* Real CLI probe: `[project]\nname=..\nversion=..\n  dependencies = [\n    "polars",\n]` -> `init` => exit=1, exc=TOMLDecodeError: Cannot overwrite a value (line 9). `_DEPENDENCIES_KEY_RE.search('[project]\n  dependencies = [\n')` returns None; `_scan_table_headers` still detects the indented [project] header correctly, so bounds is found but the array isn't.

### Ctrl+Shift+Z / Cmd+Shift+Z redo is unreachable (contradictory key+shift check) — breaks keyboard redo, the only redo shortcut on macOS
- **Location:** `frontend/src/hooks/useKeyboardShortcuts.ts:54`  ·  **confidence:** high  ·  **type:** bug  ·  bucket B195
- The redo branch requires `mod && e.key === "z" && e.shiftKey && !isTyping`. In every real browser, holding Shift changes the printable key value, so pressing Shift+Z yields `e.key === "Z"` (uppercase), never `"z"`. Therefore the condition `e.key === "z" && e.shiftKey` can never be simultaneously true: when `shiftKey` is true the key is `"Z"`. The standard redo accelerator Ctrl+Shift+Z (Windows/Linux) and Cmd+Shift+Z (macOS) is dead. On macOS there is no Ctrl+Y fallback (line 60 only helps Windows users), so keyboard redo is completely non-functional for Mac users. The undo branch at line 49 (`e.key === "z" && !e.shiftKey`) works because Ctrl alone does not alter the character.
- *Evidence:* Line 49 undo: `if (mod && e.key === "z" && !e.shiftKey && !isTyping)`. Line 54 redo: `if (mod && e.key === "z" && e.shiftKey && !isTyping)`. Browser semantics: Shift+Z => KeyboardEvent.key === "Z". So line 54 is unreachable in real browsers; Cmd+Shift+Z on Mac matches NO handler (lines 49/54/60 all fail) => nothing happens, no preventDefault. The unit test (useKeyboardShortcuts.test.ts:67-70) fire...

The dotted leaf is then interpreted by `_resolve_leaf` (lines 526-536) as a NESTED path: it walks `value.get("a")` then `.get("b")`, which is absent (the real key is the single string `"a.b"`), so the column resolves to None. - *Evidence:* Reproduced: records=[{"a.b":42,"ok":1}] -> infer column path '$[*].a.b' -> build+load root frame: column 'a.b' value = [None] (source had 42); 'ok' = [1]. _resolve_leaf({'a.b':42}, 'a.b'): '.' in leaf -> split ['a','b'] -> cur=value.get('a')=None -> returns None. ### Sliced subscripts render as raw ast.dump garbage (e.g. 'a[Slice(lower=Constant(value=1), upper=Constant(value=2))]') because convert has no ast.Slice case

For a plain index `a[0]` node.slice is a Constant (handled), but for a slice `a[1:2]` node.slice is an ast.Slice, which convert (152-186) does not dispatch on — it falls through to the `return ast.dump(node)` fallback (line 186). The resulting human-readable text contains a raw AST repr ('Slice(lower=Constant(value=1), upper=Constant(value=2))') instead of '1:2'. Polars expressions can produce subscript-with-slice (e.g. Expr.__getitem__ slicing or struct/array indexing), so this leaks internal AST text into the user-facing formula.

### `truncated` flag is a false positive when exactly max_examples distinct invalid values exist
- **Location:** `src/haute/modelling/_feature_contract.py:481`  ·  **confidence:** medium  ·  **type:** bug  ·  bucket B103
- The error context computes `truncated=len(invalid_values) >= max_examples`. But `invalid_values` is produced by `.head(max_examples)`, so `len(invalid_values)` can never exceed `max_examples`; the comparison is true precisely when `len == max_examples`. That condition is reached both when the invalid set was genuinely larger than `max_examples` (truly truncated) AND when there were exactly `max_examples` distinct invalid values with none dropped. In the latter case `truncated=True` is wrong and would mislead an operator/log consumer into believing more invalid levels exist than were reported. The standard fix is to materialise `max_examples + 1` examples and report `truncated = len(examples)...
- *Evidence:* Line 456-461 builds `... .head(max_examples) .implode()`. Line 469-473 yields at most `max_examples` items. Line 481 sets `truncated=len(invalid_values) >= max_examples`, so a column with exactly `max_examples` distinct out-of-domain values reports `truncated=True` despite the list being complete.

### removeFactor adjusts the active tab using safeIdx instead of the removed index, so deleting a tab before the active one silently moves the selection
- **Location:** `frontend/src/panels/editors/BandingEditor.tsx:110-115`  ·  **confidence:** high  ·  **type:** bug  ·  bucket B148
- removeFactor filters out `idx` then only re-clamps the active index with `if (safeIdx >= next.length) setActiveIdx(next.length - 1)` (lines 113-114). It never accounts for removing a factor positioned BEFORE the currently-active one. When `idx < safeIdx` and `safeIdx` is still < next.length, no adjustment runs, so `activeIdx` keeps its old numeric value while every factor after the removed one shifted down by one — the highlighted/edited factor silently becomes a different column.
- *Evidence:* factors [A,B,C,D], active index 2 (C). User clicks remove on tab A (idx 0). next=[B,C,D] (length 3). safeIdx=2 which is < 3, so the guard does not fire and activeIdx stays 2 => now resolves to D, not the C the user was editing. The active selection jumps to the wrong factor. Correct handling would decrement activeIdx when removing an index <= the active one.

The effect's cleanup aborts the *local* `controller` variable (`return () => { controller.abort() }`), not `abortRef.current`. The manual `fetchForPath` call path never consults `abortRef` either. The ref therefore serves no purpose and gives the misleading impression that in-flight requests are tracked/cancellable across calls when they are not.
- *Evidence:* Line 15 declares ref; line 38 creates `const controller = new AbortController()`; line 39 `abortRef.current = controller`; line 42 calls `fetchForPath(initialPath, controller.signal)`; cleanup (line 44-46) calls `controller.abort()` using the closure variable, not `abortRef.current`. Grep for `abortRef` in the file returns only the declaration (15) and the assignment (39) — zero reads.
